| H67 | Closing profile of the final build (the stop condition of the acceptance contract): the decode profile shows the dense Q8_0 matvec family `mul_mat_vec_q<Q8_0, 1..4>` at 11.7% of the kernel time, a category of 5% or more that no ledger row names | up to 3% of a decode round if it reached 85% of the card's bandwidth | measurement (bound) | RESOLVED (bounded; structural limit, no code) | Closing profiles (`build/pascal/closing-pf/`, `closing-dc/`, `h60-bin` with all defaults, the stack; analysis `build/pascal/b9a32b-closing.py`): prefill 16,384 tokens (718.6 tok/s, 21,736 ms of kernel time): `mul_mat_q` Q8_0 J64 20.8% (rows H29, H50), `flash_attn_tile` int8 QK + PV 18.7% (+2.9% for the 4-row variant; rows H28, H46), `mul_mat_q` Q4_K 10.4% in the main launch and 6.4% in the remainder launches (H30, H37, H50), `gated_delta_net_quad` 6.2% (H27, H51), `mul_mat_q` Q5_K 5.9% in the main launch (H30, H37, H50); a lump of small elementwise kernels is 6.9% in sum with no single kernel above 2.7% (`k_bin_bcast` mul). Decode, 3 chats of 128 tokens (12,613 ms of kernel time, 143 rounds = 384 tokens - 241 accepted drafts): `moe_hybrid_wait_add` 26.8% (H31 and the rows it cites), `mul_mat_vec_q` Q6_K head 6.7% + 1.8% (H8, H8b, H8c, H31: at the bandwidth limit), `mul_mat_vec_q_moe` Q4_K 5.1% + Q5_K 3.7% (H7), the Q8_0 `mul_mat_vec_q` family 11.7% (this row); the `mul_mat_q` kernels in the decode profile are the prompts of the chats and the warm-up (prefill rows). The Q8_0 family: the model's Q8_0 tensors total 1,978 MiB, of which the token embedding (515 MiB) is a row lookup, so a verify round reads 1,463 MiB of Q8_0 weights through these kernels (attention projections, the delta-net projections and the shared experts; the draft layer adds about 36 MiB); 1,471.8 ms over 143 rounds is 10.3 ms per round, i.e. 149 GB/s = 58% of the card's 256 GB/s. The kernel is the stock generic one for Pascal (4 warps, 2 rows per block for 2 to 4 columns). The structural reason for the gap, not tested as a kernel: a `block_q8_0` is 34 bytes (a 2-byte scale and 32 quants), so its 4-byte quant loads are 2-byte aligned and cannot be widened to 16-byte loads; a layout that could (36 or 48 bytes) would need a second copy of 1.46 GiB of weights next to the original, which the prefill kernels still read, and the card has no room for it (7.3 GiB used of 8). Potential if the loads were the whole gap: 10.3 ms to about 6.9 ms (85% of peak) per round of about 53 ms, of which only the attention-side part is on the critical path before the host request (the shared experts overlap the host leg), so about 1.7 ms or 3% of a round at the very best. Verdict: RESOLVED by the bound and the footprint argument, with the stated assumption that the loads, not the dot product, are the limit; no kernel was written. |
| H68 | A ceiling for the one idea D43 excludes (the second GPU as extra expert cache): how much does decode speed depend on the number of resident expert slots, and what would a perfect cache give? Measured on ONE card only | the cap of the second-GPU idea | measurement (bound) | RESOLVED (perfect-cache cap +76%; D43 stands with that bound) | Contract. `build/pascal/h60-bin`, the stack (p-min 0.7, 6 threads), short context so that the cache can be large: `--hybrid-moe-slots N` fixed (a ceiling and a floor, the prefill window is irrelevant for decode) with N in {14, 24, 34, 45, 56, the largest value that starts}, `-c 8192` (the flag is added with `--drop "-c 262000" --add "-c 8192"`), `--pp "" --chats 3 --deep 0 --tg 128 --hlog`, one run per N, GPU 0 only. Per run: the mean decode tok/s of the three chats, the mean ms per round (eval time / (tokens - accepted drafts)), the engine's hit rate and the slots actually used. The texts differ between runs, so ms per round and hit rate are the figures, tok/s beside them. Analysis fixed now: fit ms per round against the miss rate (1 - hit rate) by least squares over the runs; the intercept is the cost per round of a cache that never misses, and 1000 ms / (intercept x tokens per round) is the cap of tok/s for ANY cache size on this machine (the second card cannot beat it). The row reports the cap, the fit's R squared and the slots where the hit rate would reach 0.9 by the observed trend (an extrapolation, labelled). Verdict rule: if the cap is less than 10% above the best measured tok/s the second-GPU idea is capped below 10% and D43's remark is revised to say so; otherwise D43 stands with the cap as its upper bound. Claims allowed: the measured points and the bound by fit; no claim about a second card's real speed (PCIe and P2P costs are not in it). About 20 minutes of GPU. Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h68.sh`. Result (`H68-N.txt`, `H68-points.json`, three chats of 128 tokens per point, `-c 8192`, one card): N = 14 / 24 / 34 / 45 / 56 slots give hit rate 0.318 / 0.419 / 0.491 / 0.580 / 0.637, decode 29.55 / 31.71 / 34.69 / 37.27 / 39.58 tok/s, ms per round 94.17 / 85.27 / 79.06 / 72.55 / 68.80, tokens per round 2.78 / 2.70 / 2.74 / 2.70 / 2.72; N = 64, 72 and 80 were refused at start-up with the layer-major window on (`cudaMalloc` of the 498 MiB layer slot failed, `model loading error`), which is the window's memory, not a limit of the cache (see H68b below). Fit by least squares over the five points: ms per round = 39.20 + 79.77 x miss rate, R squared 0.997; the intercept is the cost of a round with a cache that never misses, 39.2 ms, which at 2.73 tokens per round is 69.7 tok/s: the cap for ANY cache size on this machine, +76% over the best measured point (39.58) and +87% over the 45-slot point (37.27). The hit rate gains about 0.005 per slot over the last interval (0.008 before it), falling; reaching 0.9 by the trend needs more than 100 slots (the last slope alone gives about 106). Labelled extrapolation, not measured: at 155 slots a hit rate of 0.85 to 0.90 would give about 47 to 51 ms per round, i.e. 54 to 58 tok/s, +35 to +47% over 39.58. H68b (`build/pascal/b9a32b-h68b.sh`, the same runs with the layer-major prefill and its flags switched off, so decode only): 56 slots hit 0.6146, 39.00 tok/s, 69.84 ms per round; 64 slots hit 0.6591, 40.07 tok/s, 67.96 ms per round; 72, 80, 88 and 96 slots were refused (`cudaMalloc` of 411 MiB failed), so the one-card maximum at `-c 8192` is 64 to 71 slots for decode alone. The fit of the five first points predicted 66.39 ms per round at the hit rate of the 64-slot run; the measured 67.96 is 2.4% higher, an out-of-sample check that the line holds (the same slot count gave hit rates 0.6367 and 0.6146 in the two series, so a hit rate carries about 0.02 of run-to-run spread); refit with the 64-slot point: ms per round = 40.68 + 77.25 x miss, R squared 0.995, cap 67.1 tok/s (+68% over 40.07 at 64 slots). The rule of the contract (cap above 10%): D43 stands, with this as its bound. Not in the numbers: the cost of reaching a second card's memory over PCIe, and whether the engine's per-layer cache would use 8 GiB more efficiently. |
| H69 | The +35 to +47% decode of D44 (a second card as extra expert cache, 155 slots) rests on an extrapolation of the hit rate; the engine can dump its routing (`LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_TRACE`) and `tools/analyze-pregate.py replay_policy` replays the cache policy exactly, host-only: replay the policy at slot counts one card cannot hold, after checking that the replay reproduces the hit rates measured at 14 to 64 slots | trace-driven hit rate at 96 to 256 slots | measurement (host-side replay of a recorded trace) | VALIDATED and answered (trace-driven: 57 to 60 tok/s at 155 slots against 38 to 40 at 45) | Contract. Traces: two decode-only runs on ONE card (`h60-bin`, the stack, layer-major prefill off, `-c 8192`, `--hybrid-moe-slots 64`, `--env LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_TRACE=...`): T1 = the H68 workload (`--chats 3 --tg 128`) and T2 = a longer one (`--chats 8 --tg 256`). Replay: `replay_policy(ids, prior, slots, lam=0.97, budget=16, scan_mode=2)` with a prior that ranks experts by index (the harness gives the engine no frequency file), uniform slots per layer. Validation, fixed now: the replay of T1 reproduces the measured hit rates of H68 / H68b (0.318, 0.419, 0.491, 0.580, 0.626 (mean of the two 56-slot series), 0.659 at 14, 24, 34, 45, 56, 64 slots; the measured value is cumulative from the first step) within 0.03 absolute at five of the six slot counts. If it does, the replay's hit rates at 96, 128, 155, 192 and 256 slots (T1 and T2, steps from the 8th on) are converted by the H68b line (ms per round = 40.68 + 77.25 x miss, 2.73 tokens per round) into decode tok/s and D44's extrapolated range is replaced by them, labelled as trace-driven estimates for these texts; if it does not, D44's range stays as an extrapolation and the failure is reported. Limits that stay whatever the result: three to eight short chats, no long-context traces, no cost of reading a second card's memory over PCIe, and the policy replayed is the engine's (a second card could use another one). No GPU is used for the replay. Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h69.sh` (recording, about 8 minutes of GPU) and `build/pascal/b9a32b-h69.py` (replay). Result (`build/pascal/H69-replay.txt`; the replay script is pure Python because numpy is not installed, same order of operations as `replay_policy`: ingest, publish pending, apply the scanned pairs, scan): traces T1 = 426 rows (the H68 workload), T2 = 2,224 rows (8 chats x 256 tokens). Validation, replay of T1 from step 0 against the measured cumulative hit rate: 14 slots 0.3432 against 0.3180 (+0.025), 24 slots 0.4424 against 0.4191 (+0.023), 34 slots 0.5179 against 0.4906 (+0.027), 45 slots 0.5840 against 0.5802 (+0.004), 56 slots 0.6360 against 0.6257 (+0.010), 64 slots 0.6702 against 0.6591 (+0.011): within 0.03 at 6 of 6, VALIDATED; the replay is optimistic by 0.004 to 0.027, so the predictions below are read with about 0.02 of hit rate taken off. Prediction from the 8th step on, converted by the H68b line (40.68 + 77.25 x miss ms per round, 2.73 tokens per round): 45 slots hit 0.592 / 0.641 (T1 / T2), 37.8 / 39.9 tok/s; 64 slots 0.678 / 0.728, 41.6 / 44.2; 96 slots 0.785 / 0.829, 47.7 / 50.7; 128 slots 0.860 / 0.899, 53.0 / 56.3; 155 slots 0.908 / 0.941, 57.1 / 60.4; 192 slots 0.955 / 0.973, 61.9 / 63.9; 256 slots 1.000, 67.1. A second card as extra cache (155 slots) is therefore worth +51% / +51% over 45 slots (the default at the owner's context) and +37% / +37% over 64 slots by the replay, i.e. about 55 to 58 tok/s after the 0.02 hit-rate correction against 38 to 40, about +40 to +50%; this replaces D44's extrapolated +35 to +47% by a trace-driven range of the same size. Limits: short chats only (3 and 8 prompts of 128 to 256 tokens), no long-context trace, the engine's own policy, and nothing for the cost of using a second card (activations crossing the link twice per layer, about 40 layers per round, and the extra engine); the speed conversion uses the one-card line, whose R squared is 0.995 to 0.997. |
| H70 | How much hit rate can ANY cache policy gain at today's 45 slots? The engine's online policy (decayed frequency, 16 promotions per token) gets 0.64 on trace T2 (H69); the offline-optimal replacement (Belady's MIN with bypass, perfect foresight, no promotion budget) is an upper bound for every online policy, including predictors | the headroom of policy work on one card | measurement (host-side bound) | BOUND LARGE (0.20 at 45 slots), PRACTICAL HEADROOM SMALL (about 0.02); closed | Contract. Per layer, the sequence of the experts selected in each row of trace T2 (`build/pascal/h69/t2.bin`, 2,224 rows, 40 layers, 8 used of 256); a cache of K = 45, 64 and 155 slots per layer; MIN with bypass: on a miss insert if there is room, else replace the cached expert whose next use is farthest if the newcomer's next use is sooner, otherwise bypass; the cache starts empty and the first 8 rows are left out of the count, as in H69. Bound, not a policy: it ignores the promotion budget, the copy latency and the fact that the future is unknown. Criterion, fixed now: headroom = MIN hit rate - the online replay's hit rate of H69 at the same K (T2, from the 8th step). If the headroom at 45 slots is below 0.05 (below about 5% of decode through the H68b line, 77 ms x 0.05 = 3.9 ms of a 68 ms round) policy work is CLOSED by the bound; at 0.10 or more it is a lever worth a design row; between the two the row says so and stops. The conversion of a hit-rate gain into decode is the H68b line, the same caveats. Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h70.py`. Result (the script prints every figure; traces of H69): offline-optimal MIN with bypass against the online replay, from the 8th row: T2 at 45 slots 0.838 against 0.641 (headroom +0.197, 15.2 ms of a round through the H68b line, 22%), at 64 slots 0.892 against 0.728 (+0.165), at 155 slots 0.977 against 0.941 (+0.036); T1 at 45 slots 0.831 against 0.592 (+0.240), at 64 slots +0.204, at 155 slots +0.041. By the criterion (0.10 or more) the bound is a lever worth looking at, so it was looked at with the cost side: (a) zero-latency online variants (a miss is inserted at once, no budget) reach 0.715 to 0.735 at 45 slots on T2 (LRU 0.718, decayed frequency with lambda 0.9 / 0.97 / 0.99: 0.735 / 0.715 / 0.662), but need 45 to 85 insertions per row, each a 1.9 MiB copy over the link (about 0.3 ms at 6.4 GB/s), against the 16 per token that the engine's budget allows; (b) the same decayed-frequency rule held to 16 insertions per row with zero latency reaches 0.658 (lambda 0.9) and 0.663 (lambda 0.97), only +0.017 to +0.022 over the engine's 0.641 (the whole price of the engine's lag and asynchronous copies, 1.3 to 1.7 ms of a round at most), and at 32 per row 0.688 to 0.695 (+0.05); (c) the live engine with B = 32 or lambda 0.95 / 0.98 was measured on this machine and the hit rate FELL (0.614 and 0.620 against 0.630, rounds 1 to 5% slower; guide, 'Tried and left out'), the same finding as the earlier G17c (an extra promotion costs about 24 us of link and GPU time and the token does not get shorter). Verdict: the oracle's headroom is real but belongs to knowing the future (a predictor of the next token's routing, researched before and capped by capacity, T-GPLR-58), not to any online rule at this promotion budget; the practical headroom of the rule itself is at most about 0.02 of hit rate (about 2% of a round), the latency cost, which asynchronous DMA does not allow to remove. No code was written. |
| H71 | A bound for the routing predictor of D46 / D47 on one card: how much hit rate (and so decode) can a PERFECT predictor of the next row's routing add, if it may issue only Q extra link copies per row beyond the engine's 16 promotions? | the net value of the predictor, at best | measurement (host-side bound on the H69 traces) | CEILING REPORTED (perfect oracle: net +4.7% / +8.5% / +14.9% of a round at Q = 8 / 16 / 32; with precision 0.66: +3.0% / +5.4% / +9.5%); nothing built | Contract. Trace T2 (`build/pascal/h69/t2.bin`), 45 slots per layer, 2,224 rows, from the 8th row; the base is the instant-insertion decayed-frequency rule held to 16 insertions per row (lambda 0.97), which reaches 0.663 in H70 and is the engine's rule without its lag. The oracle: before each row is served, up to Q of the (layer, expert) pairs that the row needs and the cache does not hold are inserted (the largest-gain victims by EMA), at most one per layer-expert; Q in {4, 8, 16, 32}. Net decode value per row = 77.25 ms x (hit gain) - Q x 0.024 ms (the measured cost of an extra promotion in G17c: about 24 us of link and GPU time). A realistic predictor's precision (G13: 0.66 of the top-2 non-resident predictions are true misses) scales the hit gain by at most that factor and the copies by none. Criterion, fixed now: the predictor is CLOSED by the bound if the best net value of the PERFECT oracle over Q is below 2% of a round (68 ms x 0.02 = 1.4 ms), and with the precision factor of 0.66 the real one would be smaller still; if the oracle's net value is 2% or more the row reports it as the ceiling of a design that would still have to be built and measured, and says that nothing was built. Claims allowed: a host-side ceiling on short chats (T2), not a speed. Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h71.py`. Result (`build/pascal/H71.txt`; base: the instant decayed-frequency rule at 16 insertions per row, hit 0.663): the perfect oracle with Q extra insertions per row reaches hit 0.686 / 0.706 / 0.743 / 0.804 at Q = 4 / 8 / 16 / 32 (+0.024 / +0.044 / +0.080 / +0.141), a gain of 1.8 / 3.4 / 6.2 / 10.9 ms of a 68 ms round through the H68b line, minus the copy cost of 0.024 ms per copy (0.1 / 0.2 / 0.4 / 0.8 ms): net 1.7 / 3.2 / 5.8 / 10.1 ms = 2.5% / 4.7% / 8.5% / 14.9%; scaled by the precision 0.66 of G13's top-2 predictions, 1.1 / 2.0 / 3.7 / 6.4 ms = 1.6% / 3.0% / 5.4% / 9.5%. The perfect oracle is above the 2% line, so by the criterion the predictor is NOT closed by this bound: its ceiling on short chats is 3 to 5% of a round at a realistic budget of 8 to 16 extra copies per row (2.4 to 4.8 ms of link time per row at 6.4 GB/s, see point 2 below), nothing was built. What the bound leaves out and the live record says: (1) the model charges 0.024 ms per copy, from G17c, where the token did NOT get shorter, and the live engine at B = 32 (16 more promotions per token, the same kind of traffic) lost 1 to 5% of the round with a FALLING hit rate; (2) 16 extra copies per row are 4.8 ms of link time per row at 6.4 GB/s, on top of the 4.8 ms of the engine's own 16 promotions, about 14% of the round on a link the host-expert path already uses; (3) the real predictor works from the hidden state of the layer before, so its lead is one layer period (about 1.7 ms) and every wrong or late copy is lost traffic; (4) the traces are short chats. Verdict: a ceiling of 3 to 5%, discounted by the live evidence to an unknown value that I expect to be below 2%; the work to find out (a pre-gate router node per layer in the graph, a mailbox for the predictions, copies queued ahead of use, a new failure mode in the spinning handshake of AGENTS.md) is large. Not built; listed. |
| H72 | A real expert prior for the cold start (the idea named at the end of D50): `--hybrid-moe-freq` fills the initial cache and breaks the first ties from a frequency table; the merged build lost the accidental priming of the old warm-up, so a held-out table built from routing traces should bring the first chats back to (or above) the pre-merge speed | first-chat decode +4..10% after a start | flag + tool | KEPT as advice (mean of three chats +4.4%, chat 0 +13.7%) | Contract. Tool: `tools/pascal/make_freq_prior.py` (counts per layer and expert from `LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_TRACE` traces). Prior: counts from trace T2 (8 chats x 256 tokens, H69) WITHOUT its first 834 rows (about the first three chats, which are the test chats): `build/pascal/h69/prior-t2-late.txt`, 1,390 rows from other prompts, so the test prompts are held out. Arms: `build/pascal/merged-bin`, the stack flags, `--pp "" --chats 3 --deep 0 --tg 128 --hlog` (the cold-start workload of H70's neighbour D49 / D50); A = no table (the default), B = `--hybrid-moe-freq build/pascal/h69/prior-t2-late.txt`; A-B-B-A. Reference, not an arm of the criterion: the pre-merge build `h60-bin` without a table from the MRG3 runs (38.6 tok/s). Criteria, fixed now: the prior is KEPT as advice if B beats A in decode tok/s by at least 3.0% on the mean of the three chats with no A/B overlap, chat 0 gains at least 3.0%, and no chat loses more than 2.0%; the cache hit rate is reported beside it. Text may differ between arms (a different cache changes the near-tie numerics); chats with a differing text are judged by ms per round. Claims allowed: the cold-start gain of a held-out prior on these three chats, one machine; the tool and the flag are documented. About 10 minutes of GPU. Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h72.sh`. Result (A-B-B-A, `H72.table`, merged build): chat 0 33.78 / 38.99 / 37.98 / 33.93 tok/s, mean 33.86 -> 38.48 (+13.7%, ms per round 82.19 -> 73.92, -10.1%, acceptance 0.911 -> 0.965, the text differs); chat 1 34.33 -> 34.67 (+1.0%, same text); chat 2 38.23 -> 37.97 (-0.7%, same text). Mean over the three chats of one arm: A 35.56 / 35.38, B 37.26 / 36.82, no overlap, +4.4% (35.47 -> 37.04). Whole-run cache hit rate 0.5692 -> 0.6071, peak VRAM equal. All criteria met (mean at least +3.0% without overlap, chat 0 at least +3.0%, no chat below -2.0%): KEPT. For scale: the pre-merge build had a mean of 38.6 on this test through its accidental warm-up priming, so a held-out prior built from only 1,390 rows of other prompts brings the merged build back to 37.0 (chat 0 above the pre-merge 37.5). Limits: three chats of 128 tokens, a prior built from the same kind of prompts (short chats), one machine; a prior from a different workload has not been tried. |
| H73 | How much can a WRONG expert prior hurt? H72 helped with a table built from other short chats; D51 left open that a table from another workload, or a bad one, is no worse than the uniform prior only by assumption. Two wrong tables on the same cold-start test | the risk bound of the advice | flag + tool | ANSWERED (another workload +5.4%; the adversarial table -1.4%) | Contract. Test and arms as H72 (merged build, the stack flags, `--pp "" --chats 3 --deep 0 --tg 128 --hlog`, A-B-B-A, A = no table). Table W (other domain): counts from a routing trace of a code text at 16,384 tokens of depth (`--text build/pascal/h35-code.txt --chats 0 --deep 16384 --tg 256`, recorded now with `LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_TRACE`) with `make_freq_prior.py`. Table I (adversarial): the matched table of H72 inverted, count -> (max count of the layer) - count, so the rarest experts of the chats come first. Two sessions, A against W and A against I. Criteria, fixed now: the advice 'build the table from your own traffic' stands with a stated risk if, on the mean of the three chats, W is not worse than A by more than 2.0% (a table of another workload is not harmful); I is reported as the bound of a wrong table and is not a criterion. If W is worse than A by more than 2.0% the guide says that a table from a different workload can cost that much and must be built from the same kind of traffic. Claims allowed: the two measured deltas on this test. About 15 minutes of GPU. Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h73.sh`. Result (A-B-B-A each, `H73w.table`, `H73inv.table`; table W from 277 rows of a code continuation at 16,384 tokens of depth): W: chat 0 33.91 -> 38.89 (+14.7%, ms per round -10.9%, text differs), chat 1 34.25 -> 35.94 (+4.9%, text differs, ms per round +1.1%), chat 2 38.23 -> 37.34 (-2.3%, text differs, ms per round +0.2%); mean of the three chats 35.46 -> 37.39 (+5.4%), hit rate 0.5692 -> 0.6052. Criterion (W not worse than A by more than 2.0%): met, W is better. I (the matched table inverted): chat 0 33.67 -> 32.08 (-4.8%, ms per round +7.3%, text differs), chat 1 34.09 -> 34.09 (0.0%, same text), chat 2 38.03 -> 38.09 (+0.2%, same text); mean 35.26 -> 34.75 (-1.4%), hit rate 0.5692 -> 0.5439. Reading: a table from a different domain helped as much as the matched one (+5.4% against +4.4%), so what it carries is the model's general expert popularity more than the workload's; the worst table I could build (the rarest experts first) cost 1.4% on this cold-start test, all of it in the first chat, and nothing once the engine had its own statistics (chats 1 and 2 are equal). Limits: the same three short chats, one machine, one wrong table of each kind. |
| H74 | The second GPU as an extra expert-cache tier (D43-D45): what does serving the missed experts of one layer on GPU 1 really cost per request? The replay model of H69 assumed a GPU 1 hit is as cheap as a GPU 0 hit; the activation must cross to GPU 1 and the result back, and a request is a handful of small kernels | the latency L1(m) of a GPU 1 request, the number the +40..50% estimate lacks | measurement (microbenchmark) | CRITERION MET (m = 4: 106 us against the 350 us bar) | Contract. Authority: the owner answered 'Yes, for this experiment only' to the question whether GPU 1 may be used for it (AskUserQuestion, 2026-10-31); the one-GPU rule stays for everything else, the experiment code is opt-in and the default stays one GPU. Tool: `tools/pascal/gpu1_serve_bench.cpp` (a ggml graph on the CUDA backend of device 1: copy x, expert slots and router weights in, MUL_MAT_ID up / gate, swiglu, MUL_MAT_ID down, weighted sum, copy 2048 floats out; 110 slots of one layer; Q4_K gate / up, Q5_K down; m = 1..8 experts; 3,000 requests each; wall clock per request). Reference: the host leg serves a request in 563 us on average at the observed miss mix (lean team stages, D50 run: 8 + 326 + 11 + 220 us; mean m about 4.4) and 700 to 790 us as the engine measures it. Criteria, fixed now: the idea goes on to a trace-driven model of the whole decode (and then to an integrated prototype) if the mean latency of a request with m = 4 experts on GPU 1 is at most 350 us (0.6 of the host leg); between 350 and 560 us it goes on only if a lower-level implementation (raw CUDA calls instead of a ggml graph) is argued to reach 350 in the row; above 560 us it is closed, because GPU 1 would then be no faster than the CPU it is meant to relieve. Claims allowed: the latency of this microbenchmark on this machine, not a decode speed. Command: `build/pascal/gpu1_serve_bench 1 110 3000`. Result (GPU 1 idle, 110 slots of one layer, 3,000 requests per m; mean / p99, microseconds): m = 1 65.2 / 69.5; 2 79.2 / 84.2; 3 91.3 / 95.1; 4 106.1 / 110.3; 5 122.0 / 137.6; 6 136.4 / 140.1; 7 151.5 / 155.8; 8 167.5 / 181.8; a line of 50.6 us + 14.6 us per expert. At m = 4 that is 0.19 of the host leg's 563 us, far under the 350 us bar, through a plain ggml graph (launch and synchronisation included, no raw CUDA yet). It leaves out what an integration adds: the hop of the request from the GPU 0 mailbox to the thread that calls GPU 1, contention with GPU 0's own work on the host side, and the promotions into GPU 1. The next step is the model of H75. |
| H75 | A trace-driven model of the decode with a GPU 1 tier whose requests cost what H74 measured (50.6 us + 14.6 us per expert) instead of nothing: how much of the estimated +40..50% (H69, D45) survives? | the modelled decode gain of a 110-slot second tier | measurement (model on recorded traces) | CRITERION MET (N1 = 110: +40.8% / +35.8%) | Contract. Trace T2 (`build/pascal/h69/t2.bin`, 2,224 rows, 8 chats x 256 tokens), from the 8th row. Baseline: tier 0 only, 45 slots per layer, the replay of the engine policy (hit 0.641). Variants: a combined cache of 45 + N1 slots per layer for N1 in {32, 64, 110}, membership by the same policy replay (budget 16 promotions per row for each card's link, which is optimistic for the second card's host link: both cards read the same host memory), tier 0 = the 45 cached experts of a layer with the highest EMA, tier 1 = the others. Per layer and row the request cost is c = max(c_host(n_miss), c_g1(n_t1)), zero for an empty set; c_g1(m) = 50.6 + 14.6 m us (H74); c_host(m) in two forms that bracket the unknown split of the host cost into a fixed and a per-expert part: proportional (128 m us) and affine (200 us + 80 m us), each scaled by one factor kappa so that the baseline reproduces the measured 68.4 ms per round (40.68 + 77.25 x miss rate of H68b at hit 0.641, 2.73 rows per round, floor A = 40.68 ms per round). The speed ratio is (A + wait_base) / (A + wait_variant). Criteria, fixed now: the idea goes on to an integrated prototype if the modelled gain for N1 = 110 is at least +15% in BOTH host-cost forms; between +5% and +15% it goes on only if the row argues a cheaper integration; below +5% it is closed. The model leaves out the promotion traffic of the second link, the CPU time of the thread that serves GPU 1, and every effect of the spinning handshake; its gain is therefore an upper bound. Claims allowed: the modelled ratios on the recorded traces. Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h75.py`. Result (baseline hit 0.641, modelled round 68.4 ms): proportional host cost (kappa 0.691): N1 = 32 -> resident hit 0.774, round 58.9 ms, +16.1%; N1 = 64 -> 0.860, 53.2 ms, +28.5%; N1 = 110 -> 0.941, 48.6 ms, +40.8% (tier 1 serves 23.0% of all lookups); affine host cost (kappa 0.627): +12.2% / +22.8% / +35.8%. Criterion (N1 = 110 at least +15% in both forms): MET, by a wide margin, so the idea goes on to an integrated prototype. Reading: charging a GPU 1 hit its measured latency removes only about a tenth of the +45% of the cost-free replay (D45); the cause is that one request to GPU 1 covers all tier-1 experts of a layer at once (50.6 us + 14.6 us per expert) and runs in parallel with the host leg. The model is an upper bound: no promotion traffic for the second link, no CPU time of the thread that serves GPU 1, no handshake effects. |
| H76 | CRITERION MET (D: +29.4% / +24.0%, 22.5 copies per row) | A realistic policy for the GPU 1 tier, replayed offline before any engine code (H75 let the tier follow the EMA rank of one combined cache, which hides the cost of moving experts between tiers). Trace T2 (`build/pascal/h69/t2.bin`), from the 8th row. Tier 0: the engine's policy at 45 slots, unchanged (EMA 0.97, budget 16, scan 8, one-row lag). Tier 1: N1 slots per layer, disjoint from tier 0 at the moment of filling, filled only by copies from the host: (S) a refresh every P rows (P = 8, 32, 128) sets the tier to the N1 experts of highest EMA that are not in tier 0 (cached or pending), the copies land one row later; (D) every row at most B1 = 25 swaps, a swap = the best uncached non-tier-0 expert for the worst tier-1 expert while its EMA is higher, also one row later. Per row and layer the time is max(host(misses), 50.6 + 14.6 m1 us) as in H75, both host forms, the same kappa calibration. Reported: ratio to the 45-slot baseline for N1 = 32, 64, 110, and the steady-state copies per row to GPU 1 (an expert is 1.6875 MiB; the first fill is reported apart). Criterion: at N1 = 110 the best S setting AND the D policy each keep at least +15% in both host forms with at most 25 expert copies per row after the first fill; if only D passes, the engine takes D, if only S passes, S, if neither, the idea needs a different tier policy (the plan records which). Command: `build/pascal/b9a32b-h76.py`, output `build/pascal/h76.txt`. Result (baseline hit 0.641; steady = after the first 128 rows; copies are expert copies host -> GPU 1 per row): N1 = 110: S8 hit 0.935, +38.6% / +32.9%, 28.3 copies per row (over the limit of 25); S32 0.909, +35.1% / +28.3%, 24.4; S128 0.865, +29.0% / +21.6%, 14.8; D 0.874, +29.4% / +24.0%, 22.5. N1 = 64: S32 +24.1% / +18.3% (31.6 copies), D +13.4% / +9.9%; N1 = 32: S8 +18.1% / +13.4% (47.9), D +4.8% / +3.2%. Criterion (N1 = 110, +15% in both forms, at most 25 copies per row): S32, S128 and D all pass. Reading: moving experts between tiers and bounding the copies costs about a third of H75's +40.8% / +35.8% (realistic +24..+35% instead of +36..+41%); tier size matters more than the refresh rule (N1 = 64 gives half of N1 = 110). 25 copies per row is 42 MiB, about 1.7 GB/s at 40 rows per second, from host memory that the host leg also reads: this is the next thing to measure in the engine. |
| H77 | CRITERION MET for the idea (decode +14.5% over 8 chats, +11.4% at 32k depth), model not reached (it said +24..35%) | The GPU 1 expert tier built into the hybrid engine (opt-in `LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_G1_SLOTS=N|auto`, policy D of H76, 99 slots on the 8 GiB card), measured against the same binary with the tier off. First version (requests as three copy-engine copies plus a synchronised read-back): 289 us per request, decode 32.6 against 34.2 tok/s (-4.7%), because the small copies queued behind the bulk promotion copies of GPU 1's copy engine (the head-of-line blocking of G23, now on the second GPU). Final version: the request is read by a kernel from the pinned mailbox, the result and its completion flag are written back by a kernel, nothing waits (54 us per request). Self-check (`LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_G1_CHECK=1`): 48,936 tier-1 results compared with the lean host leg, 27 above 5% relative error, largest 7.8% (the CUDA q8_1 against CPU q8_K rounding, not a wrong expert). Tier 1 serves 29.1% of all lookups, the main cache 59.3%, resident hit rate 0.884. Open: the cost of threads (6 team threads + decoding thread + G1 thread on 8 logical CPUs), swap budget, deadlock stress, longer contexts. |

| H79 | PARTIAL: clauses 1 and 2 met, clause 3 NOT met (4 of 6 divergences under 0.3 nats) | Does the second-GPU tier move the decode more than the engine already moves it? `tools/pascal/g1_logprob_cmp.py`: 8 raw-text prompts, 96 greedy tokens each (`n_probs` 3), on three servers started one after the other: ctl (tuned one-GPU command), g1 (`--hybrid-moe-gpu1 auto`, 4 team threads, 40 swaps), ctl2 (ctl again). The claim this can support: the tier changes decode numerics no more than the engine's own placement noise. It supports no claim about answer quality. Result (`build/pascal/g1lp/`): ctl against ctl2 is bit-identical (700 tokens, all differences 0). ctl against g1: 419 identical-prefix tokens, median |dlogprob| 0.0009, mean 0.0104, p95 0.0594, max 0.1274; 6 of 8 prompts diverge within 96 tokens, at margins 0.107, 0.365, 0.012, 0.008, 0.066, 0.331 nats (four under 0.3, two just above), so the third clause fails; the criterion is not changed after seeing this. Reading: the per-token drift is small, but whether the number of divergences is more than any placement change causes is not shown by this run, which has no reference for a placement change; H79b adds one. |

| H80 | REJECTED (mean 1.000, deep 0.977; criterion +5%) | CUDA graphs for the decode on Pascal, again, now that the second-GPU tier removed most of the GPU's wait. The 2026-10-24 test (guide section 7: decode 0.991 to 0.998 of without) was taken while the GPU still waited on the CPU leg, and the b12004 sync brought back upstream's `cc < Volta` switch-off. Opt-in switch `GGML_CUDA_GRAPHS_PASCAL=1` skips that check. Measurement: A-B-B-A, tuned tier-1 command both arms (4 team threads, 40 swaps, auto slots), arm B with the switch, 8 chats x 256 tokens + 256 tokens after 32k. Criterion: mean decode of the 8 chats at least +5% and the deep decode not below -2%, no timeout / CUDA error in any server log, and the nsys idle share (same profile command) drops by at least 10 points. If it holds the switch becomes a recorded default decision for cc < Volta with the tier on; if it is between -2% and +5% the switch stays opt-in and unclaimed. Result (`H80a.table`, `b9a32b-h80.sh`): per chat B/A 1.016, 1.005, 1.026, 0.972, 0.973, 1.034, 0.973, 0.996 (mean 1.000), deep 32k 0.977; no error in either server log. Root cause (measured, not assumed): GPU 0's 4,300-node decode graph WAS already captured and replayed (trace `LLAMA_CUDA_GRAPH_TRACE=1`: compatible, properties unchanged, warm-up complete, 9 chunk instances; the b12004 sync's `cc < Volta` check applies only to graphs that are not captured, and the 2026-10-24 finding of ~1.0 stands). The switch changes only the second card's request graphs (graph launch 39.9 -> 14.8 us), which does not reach the end-to-end time. The idle that nsys-token reports (44%) is not launch overhead inside the layer loop: gaps under 0.5 ms add up to 1.4 ms per period. It is the window between steps (below). The switch stays opt-in and unclaimed. |

| H87 | RESOLVED (bounded; not built) | Idea (a) of D62, the MTP layer's experts in GPU 1 instead of GPU 0. Bound from measured slopes: the 432 MiB are six main-cache slots on GPU 0 or six tier slots on GPU 1 (73 MiB each). A tier slot is worth +0.3% (H86: 5 slots, +1.5%); a main slot is worth at most about the same or a little more (a hit costs no round trip), so moving the experts gives back about six main slots (+1.8..2.4%) and takes six tier slots (-1.8%), net about +0..0.6%, minus the two cross-device hops per draft step (about 0.4% of a round). It also needs the hybrid engine to accept two CUDA backends in the scheduler (`moe_hybrid_get` aborts) and the draft context's cross-device copies. Expected net within the noise of an A-B-B-A (0.5..1%) for a scheduler change; not built. |
| H88 | RESOLVED (bounded; not built) | Idea (b) of D62, the resident-expert matvec `mul_mat_vec_q_moe` (23.6% of the kernel time in the tier profile: Q4_K 14.0%, Q5_K 9.6%). `test-backend-ops perf -o MUL_MAT_ID -b CUDA0 -p "n_used=8.*m=512.*k=2048"` (this build, GTX 1070): Q4_K, 256 experts, n = 1 token: 43.6 us for 8 experts (4.7 MB, 108 GB/s); n = 4: 105.6 us for 32 pairs (about 17.6 MB, 167 GB/s). The card streams about 190..200 GB/s (H67), so the headroom is 15% at n = 4 and 35% at n = 1, and at n = 1 about 15 us of the 43.6 are launch and wave tail that no kernel removes. Applied to the 8 ms per step that the two kernels take, a perfect kernel saves at most 1.2..2 ms of a 48 ms round (2.5..4%); H7's rows-per-block knob measured 0.7%. Below any bar a rewrite could clear; not built. |
| H89 | RESOLVED (design-level; not run) | Idea (d) of D62, prefill on both GPUs. A layer pipeline over segments would put the KV of layers 20..39 on GPU 1, and the decode then has to run those layers on GPU 1 as well: the decode graph would span two CUDA backends, which the hybrid engine refuses (`moe_hybrid_get`), and its mailboxes, publish and wait kernels belong to one GPU's stream. Prompts under two segments (about 32k tokens) cannot overlap at all, and the layer-major pass has no overlap inside a segment. That is a redesign of the engine and the pass, with a gain only for prompts above ~32k, not an experiment. Not started. |
| H90 | NOT MET (mean -0.2%, deep +5.3%; criterion to build: mean +1.5%) | Idea (a) of D62 measured instead of bounded (H87 had only a bound). The upside of putting the MTP layer's experts in GPU 1 is the six main-cache slots it gives back on GPU 0, paid with six tier slots on GPU 1. Emulation without any scheduler change and without the cross-device hops (so an upper bound of the idea): the experts stay in GPU 0, `-fitt 80` instead of 512 hands the 432 MiB to the main cache, and `LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_G1_RESERVE_MIB=782` instead of 350 takes the same 432 MiB (about six slots) from the tier. A-B-B-A, full D61 stack with reserve 350 in arm A, 8 chats x 256 tokens + 256 tokens after 32k, GPU 1 memory sampled. Criterion to BUILD the idea: mean decode at least +1.5% (the hops cost about 0.4% and the scheduler change is not free) and deep not below -2%, with slot counts in the logs showing main cache about 6 higher and tier 5-6 lower. If the mean is below +1.5% the idea is closed by this measurement. Result (`H90a.table`, `b9a32b-h90.sh`, `H90.gpu*.mem`): main cache 45 -> 50 slots (hit rate 0.59 -> 0.63), tier 104 -> 98; per chat B/A 0.961 1.016 1.021 0.986 1.021 0.976 1.000 1.005, mean tok/s 53.22 -> 53.12 (-0.2%), deep 38.22 -> 40.23 (+5.3%, both pairs agree: 37.95 / 38.49 against 39.83 / 40.63); GPU 0 peak 7,739 MiB, GPU 1 peak 7,815 MiB; no error. This is an upper bound of idea (a): it has no cross-device hop and no scheduler change, and it still misses the bar on the 8-chat mean. The deep gain says that main-cache slots are worth more at depth; it is a single A-B-B-A and not enough to build a scheduler change for. Idea (a) is closed by measurement. |
| H91 | NOT MET (mean -0.1%, deep -2.1%) | Idea (b) of D62 measured end to end: `GGML_CUDA_MMVQ_MOE_ROWS=4` (the test knob kept by H7; 4 rows per block for the cached-expert matvec) on the full stack. H7 measured only the kernel (n = 4: Q4_K 103.9 -> 102.0 us, Q5_K 138.8 -> 127.9 us) and bounded the round at 0.7%; the verify mix is different with the tier. A-B-B-A, full D61 stack with reserve 350 in both arms, arm B with the knob, 8 chats x 256 tokens + 256 tokens after 32k. Criterion: mean decode at least +1.0% and deep not below -2%; if met the knob becomes advice (and the default question goes to a second session), if not (b) is closed for tuning knobs and what remains is a kernel rewrite bounded at 2.5..4% (H88). Result (`H91a.table`): per chat B/A 1.010 0.989 0.953 0.988 1.014 1.022 1.020 1.000, mean 52.67 -> 52.60 (-0.1%), deep 39.59 -> 38.75 (-2.1%). The knob does nothing end to end on this stack; (b) is closed for tuning knobs. |
| H92 | CRITERION MET (ideal x1.55 at 16k, x1.73 at 32k with the conservative cost; build) | Idea (d) of D62 with a measured input instead of a design-level reason: prefill on both GPUs by sequence split. Design under test: GPU 0 sweeps the first s tokens layer by layer; GPU 1 sweeps the tokens s..N one layer behind it, taking the K/V and recurrent state of each layer from GPU 0 as soon as that layer is done (both stream their own copy of every expert bank over their own PCIe link; the prefill is compute-bound at 500-600 tok/s, the 16.9 GiB of banks cost ~1.5 s per pass). The compute of GPU 1's part is exactly t(N) - t(s) of the single-GPU prefill (later tokens attending over the earlier context). Input: single-GPU cold prefill time t(N) of the full D61 stack at N = 4,096 / 8,192 / 16,384 / 24,576 / 32,768 / 49,152 (`moe_bench.py --pp`), linearly interpolated for s. Ideal two-GPU time T2(N) = min over s of max(t(s), t(N) - t(s)) + t(N)/40 (one layer of lag) + the K/V and state hand-off at 6 GB/s + 1.5 s to re-fill the tier that cannot share GPU 1's 8 GiB with the prefill (about 7.2 GiB at 6 GB/s). Criterion to BUILD (an engine redesign: the pass, the KV placement and the decode graph all change): T1/T2 at least 1.4 at N = 16,384 and at least 1.5 at N = 32,768. Otherwise the idea is closed by this measurement. Result (`h92-pp`, cold single-GPU prefill of the full D61 stack, tokens -> seconds): 4,096 -> 7.11, 8,192 -> 11.76, 16,384 -> 21.96, 24,576 -> 34.64, 32,768 -> 50.51, 49,152 -> 88.24 (576 .. 746 .. 557 tok/s; the fixed cost f = 2.46 s from the first two points). Ideal two-GPU time with the best split, one layer of lag, the hand-off at 6 GB/s and the 1.2 s tier re-fill: N = 4,096 x1.41, 8,192 x1.57, 16,384 x1.71, 24,576 x1.77, 32,768 x1.81, 49,152 x1.85; if GPU 1's part also pays the fixed cost f: x1.15, 1.36, 1.55, 1.67, 1.73, 1.80. Bar x1.4 at 16k and x1.5 at 32k: met on both readings, so the idea is to be built. The old 16k profile (`pp-nsys`, before the later prefill work) splits as flash attention 20.5%, mmq Q4_K 20.2%, Q8_0 16.4%, Q5_K 14.5%, delta net 9.8%: the expert FFN alone is about 40%, which caps a split by stage (attention and state on GPU 0, expert FFN on GPU 1) at about x1.67 before overheads and needs the scheduler to run two graphs in flight; the split by token range balances at x1.7..1.8 and moves state instead (H93). |
| H93 | CRITERION MET (bit-identical logits, state 72.8 MiB at 512 tokens) | Milestone 1 of the two-GPU prefill that H92's bar asks to build (the sequence split: GPU 0 sweeps the first s tokens, GPU 1 the rest one layer behind, taking each layer's K/V and recurrent state from GPU 0): the invariant it rests on, tested without any pipelining. `tools/pascal/seqsplit_probe.cpp`: two model instances (CUDA0, CUDA1; experts on the host as in the server), a prompt of 1,024 tokens of `build/ctx100k/base.txt`; the first 512 tokens are decoded on GPU 0, the sequence state is exported (`llama_state_seq_get_data_ext`, flags 0: attention K/V and the delta-net state) and imported into the GPU 1 context, the other 512 are decoded there; the logits of the last token are compared with one whole-prompt decode on GPU 0. References measured in the same run: whole prompt on GPU 0 twice (run-to-run floor) and whole prompt on GPU 1 (cross-GPU floor). Criterion: the import is accepted, argmax equal, and max |dlogit| and KL of the split run at most twice those of the cross-GPU floor (or both under 0.05 and 1e-4 if the floor is zero). If it fails, the state hand-off is not exact and the sequence split is closed; if it holds, milestone 2 is the per-layer version inside the pass. Result (`build/pascal/seqsplit_probe`, 1,024 tokens, split at 512, both GPUs): whole prompt on GPU 0 twice: max |dlogit| 0, KL 0; GPU 0 against GPU 1: 0, 0; the split run (512 tokens on GPU 0, `llama_state_seq_get_data_ext` 76,362,740 bytes = 72.8 MiB imported by the GPU 1 context, 512 tokens there) against the whole prompt: max |dlogit| 0.00000, mean 0, KL 0, argmax equal. So the K/V and the delta-net state of a sequence move between two model instances on two GPUs without any change of the result (the same kernels run on both cards); the run-to-run and cross-GPU floors are exactly zero, so the pipelined version can be held to bit equality. This proves the hand-off, not the pipeline or any speed. |
| H94 | CRITERIA (i)-(iv) MET without the tier (x1.42 at 16k, x1.56 at 32k, bit-identical); M2.3 (the tier's memory) is H95 | Milestone 2 of the two-GPU prefill: the sequence split inside the layer-major pass (`llama_context::lm_run`, `src/llama-lm-prefill.cpp`). Design: a second model + context on GPU 1 (the follower, created by the server from the same parameters with `devices = {CUDA1}`, a small context, no hybrid engine, lm-prefill enabled); for a pass that qualifies (fresh sequence, p0 = 0, one segment, flash attention, K/V not transposed, at least `peer_min` tokens) both contexts apply the memory of ALL chunks, the leader computes chunks [0, kc) and the follower chunks [kc, n) in a thread of its own (it also does the head, so the logits are the follower's); after the leader finishes layer il for its chunks it publishes il; the follower, before layer il, waits for it and pulls the leader's K/V cells of that layer (attention layers) or the recurrent cell (delta-net layers) with tensor copies through pinned host memory; at the end the leader pulls the follower's K/V cells and final recurrent state and the logits. kc is chosen from the measured cost model of H92 (`t(N)-t(s)` equal to `t(s)`). A pass that does not qualify runs as before. Steps: M2.0 the server flag `--lm-prefill-peer-gpu N` creates the follower context and nothing else changes (check: start-up works, GPU 1 memory logged, a normal request is bit-identical to the same server without the flag); M2.1 the peer pass for the 4k / 8k / 16k tokens of `build/ctx100k/base.txt`; M2.2 timing; M2.3 co-existence with the tier (its reserve). Acceptance (all of it, before any speed claim): (i) the first-token logprob list of the peer pass is bit-identical to the single-GPU pass for prompts of 4,096, 8,192 and 16,384 tokens and the greedy continuation of 32 tokens is identical (H93 showed that the hand-off itself is exact and the floors are 0, so equality is the bar, not a tolerance); (ii) realised prefill speedup at least x1.3 at 16,384 and at least x1.4 at 32,768 tokens (about 80% of H92's conservative ideal x1.55 / x1.73), A-B-B-A against the same server without the flag; (iii) 20 consecutive passes (mixed lengths) without error, hang or leak (GPU 0 and GPU 1 memory flat); (iv) a pass that does not qualify, or any failure of the follower before its first output, falls back to the single-GPU pass with a warning and the right result. Kill criterion: if (i) cannot be met after the follower and the hand-off are shown correct in isolation (bit-equal per layer against a dump), or if (ii) is below x1.15 at 32,768 once the pass is wired, the sequence split is closed with the measured cause. Interim result (`tools/pascal/split_check.py`, `build/pascal/split/{ref,peer}.json`, server with `-b 16384`, the owner's MTP + hybrid command, no tier, second model on CUDA1 with `--lm-prefill-peer-gpu 1 --lm-prefill-peer-min 2048`): the server starts with the follower (GPU 1 holds 2.9 GiB idle, 4.6 GiB during a pass) and a normal request is unchanged; the first batch of a prompt (fresh sequence, p0 = 0) runs split: for prompts of 4,096 / 8,192 / 16,384 tokens the 32 generated tokens and all their top-5 logprobs are bit-identical to the single-GPU pass (criterion (i), at these three sizes, for p0 = 0 only), no error line in either log, prefill 7.15 -> 9.20 s (x0.78: the follower's first pass pays one-time kernel loading), 11.29 -> 10.35 s (x1.09), 20.64 -> 14.61 s (x1.41). Needed on the way: the CUDA backend's input-batching state made thread-local (it was process-global: a latent race with the tier's thread too), the follower's graphs kept off the hybrid expert path (`llama_model::no_hybrid`), its KV cache off the process-global lazy commit, and its expert banks registered one layer at a time (a second pin of the whole file exceeds what the driver allows: it counts the same pages twice and the machine has 30 GiB). Final result (`tools/pascal/split_check.py`, `b9a32b-h94.sh`, `b9a32b-h94f.sh`; server `-b 16384`, owner's MTP + hybrid command, no tier): p0 > 0 works through the sequence-state hand-off at the start of the pass (105 MiB at 4k earlier tokens, 191 MiB at 12k: 67-115 ms to export), the follower is warmed up at start. (i) bit-identical tokens and top-5 logprobs for 4,096 / 8,192 / 16,384 / 32,768 tokens, and for 12 prompts of 5,000..31,000 tokens run in the same order on both servers (12 of 12; more than 20 split passes). Equality holds for identical request histories only: the prompt tail (the last 516 tokens) takes the ordinary path with the hybrid expert cache, which the earlier requests shape, so two runs with different histories differ in the last digits even without the split (shown: the same 16k prompt, -0.8048 and -0.8095). (ii) A-B-B-A at 16,384 tokens 21.25 / 21.47 s against 15.01 / 14.98 s = x1.424 and at 32,768 tokens 48.45 / 50.22 s against 31.54 / 31.93 s = x1.555 (bars x1.3 and x1.4). Per size in the same-order mixed run: 5,000 x0.83, 6,000 x1.05, 8,192 x1.17, 9,000 x1.16, 10,000 x1.24, 12,000 x1.30, 16,384 x1.46-1.50, 20,000 x1.35, 24,576 x1.40, 31,000 x1.59, so the default minimum of a split pass is 8,192 tokens. (iii) 36 requests (24 + 12) with more than 40 split passes, no error, no hang, GPU 1 at 2,971 MiB after every request (peak 4,619 MiB in a pass), GPU 0 7,241-7,293 MiB: flat. (iv) `LLAMA_LM_PEER_FAIL=12` (the follower fails before layer 12): the leader computes the rest, result bit-identical to the reference at x0.97 of its speed; `LLAMA_LM_FAIL_RUN=2` + `LLAMA_LM_FAIL_AT=2` (the leader fails after one layer): that request returns HTTP 500, nothing deadlocks, the next requests run split normally. Split fraction (the leader's share of the tokens): 0.40 / 0.45 / 0.50 give the same split (4 of 8 chunks) and the best time, 0.55 and 0.60 are 6-7% slower. Why the owner's `-b 4096` does not benefit: each GPU must stream all 40 banks (16.9 GiB) per pass over its x8 link, about 2.6 s, and half of a 4,096-token pass computes in about 1.9 s, so the split is copy-bound (x0.75 measured); batches of 16,384 tokens are compute-bound per GPU and need `-b 16384`. Kept out of the 1.4-1.6x: the 516-token prompt tail and the 32-token decode, which run on one GPU. |
| H95 | PARTLY MET (decode +21.6% / +19.3%, prefill x1.51 at 16k; x1.20 at 32k fails: the follower's context was too small) | M2.3 of the two-GPU prefill: GPU 1 shared by the follower (2.9 GiB idle, 4.6 GiB in a pass with a 33,024-token context; less with `--lm-prefill-peer-ctx 16896`) and the expert tier (73 MiB per slot over 40 layers; 7.2 GiB at the default reserve). They cannot both be full size, so the tier is shrunk through `LLAMA_MOE_HYBRID_G1_RESERVE_MIB=4900` (about 40 slots) before the follower is created. Measurement: one A-B-B-A over the whole thing, arm A = the tuned one-GPU command (the owner's `-b 4096`, segment 4096, 6 team threads), arm B = the D61 stack (`--hybrid-moe-gpu1 auto --mtp-draft-vocab 131072`, MTP experts in GPU 0, 4 team threads, 40 swaps) + reserve 4900 + `--lm-prefill-peer-gpu 1 --lm-prefill-peer-ctx 16896` + `-b 16384 --lm-prefill-segment 16384`; per arm: cold prefill of 16,384 and 32,768 tokens (`moe_bench.py --pp`), 8 chats x 256 tokens and 256 tokens after 32k of context. Criterion: prefill at least x1.3 at 16,384 and x1.4 at 32,768 tokens, decode mean over the 8 chats and the deep decode each at least +15% over A, GPU 0 peak not above A's by more than 150 MiB, GPU 1 peak at most 7,990 MiB, no error in any log. If only the prefill criteria hold, the combined mode is documented as a trade (the numbers decide which to recommend); if the decode criteria fail by a wide margin, the two features are documented as alternatives for the second card. Result (`H95a.table`, `b9a32b-h95.sh`, tier reserve 4900 MiB = 42 slots, follower context 16,896): prefill 16,384 tokens 724.9 -> 1,096.4 tok/s (x1.512), 32,768 tokens 642.2 -> 773.4 tok/s (x1.204); decode per chat B/A 1.136 1.246 1.265 1.226 1.185 1.226 1.254 1.194, mean tok/s 36.11 -> 43.90 (+21.6%), deep 27.79 -> 33.15 (+19.3%), prompt-processing of the deep context 638.6 -> 759.9 tok/s; GPU 0 peak 7,341 MiB (A: 7,309), GPU 1 peak 7,599 MiB. Root cause of the 32k miss (from the B logs): the second pass of a 32k prompt starts at position 16,384 and needs the whole sequence in the follower's 16,896-token context, so the follower refused, the leader recomputed its part (2 warnings per arm, 'the split pass failed on the follower') and only the first pass profited. Not a defect of the split: the split must not be attempted when p0 + n exceeds the follower's context, and a 32k prompt needs `--lm-prefill-peer-ctx` of at least 33,024. Both fixed in H95b. |
| H95b | CRITERION MET (prefill x1.49 at 16k, x1.58 at 32k; decode +20.6% / +19% deep) | H95 again with the two fixes: the leader splits only when p0 + n + 64 fits the follower's context (no wasted attempt), and the follower's context is 33,024 tokens (+180 MiB on GPU 1; the tier reserve stays 4900 MiB, so the tier keeps about 40 slots and GPU 1 should peak near 7,780 MiB). Same A-B-B-A, same criteria as H95: prefill x1.3 at 16,384 and x1.4 at 32,768 tokens, decode mean and deep decode each at least +15%, GPU 0 peak within 150 MiB of A's, GPU 1 peak at most 7,990 MiB, no error line in any log (the 'split pass failed' warning counts as an error here). Result (`H95c.table`, `b9a32b-h95b.sh`, tier reserve 4,900 MiB = 42 slots, follower context 33,024, `-b 16384 --lm-prefill-segment 16384`): prefill 16,384 tokens 742.8 -> 1,103.3 tok/s (x1.485), 32,768 tokens 661.0 -> 1,041.4 tok/s (x1.576), the 32k deep context 662.5 -> 1,036.2 tok/s (x1.564); decode per chat B/A 1.133 1.231 1.272 1.220 1.203 1.191 1.214 1.192, mean tok/s 36.27 -> 43.74 (+20.6%), deep 27.96 -> 33.22 (+18.8%); GPU 0 peak 7,341 MiB (A: 7,309), GPU 1 peak 7,783 MiB (tier 42 slots + follower), no error or 'split pass failed' line in either B log. Compared with the D61 stack without the follower (H83: decode +38.6% / +38.3% / +32.9%, prefill unchanged) the second card is traded: the follower takes 2.9-4.6 GiB of it, the tier shrinks from 104 to 42 slots, decode gives back about 18 points and prefill gains x1.5-1.6 at 16k+ tokens. Which to run is the owner's call: long prompts (documents, code bases, agent turns above ~10k tokens) favour the follower, short chats the tier. One observation without a cause: in the first launch of this A-B-B-A the server of arm A1 received a shutdown request while the 32k decode was in flight and did not exit (36 minutes, the harness waited on the open request); the chain was stopped by its PIDs and rerun, and 4 further arms and all earlier chains ended normally. The origin of the signal is not in the logs; it is recorded here as an unexplained one-off, not as a defect of the split (that arm ran the one-GPU path). |
| H96 | CRITERION MET (all exits 1.2-1.6 s with a streaming client); the one-off is explained, its trigger is not | The unexplained one-off of H95b: a one-GPU server that got a shutdown request during a 32k decode and did not exit (36 min). `tools/pascal/sigint_check.py`: start the server, send a request that needs a long generation (32k-token prompt, 512 tokens), and send SIGINT while the generation is running (3 s, then 8 s after the first streamed token); measure the time to exit. Configurations: (1) the tuned one-GPU command, (2) the D61 stack with the tier, (3) D61 + tier shrunk + the follower, each twice. Criterion: every server exits within 30 s of the signal (the harness's own wait is 60 s). If one does not, the cause is looked for before anything else is done (what the threads wait for), because that would be a defect of the engine's shutdown, not of the split. Result (`tools/pascal/sigint_check.py`, `build/pascal/sigint/`): streaming client, SIGINT 3 s and 8 s after the first token of a 32k-context generation: tuned one-GPU command 1.1 s and 31.3 s (rc 0), tier stack 1.3 and 1.2 s, tier + follower 1.5 and 1.6 s; so the threads of the tier and of the follower shut down cleanly. The 31.3 s of the one-GPU rep 1 and the 36-minute stall are the same thing: the log shows the shutdown request, then exactly 30 s later `stop: cancel task` (the HTTP side gives up on the open request after 30 s), and with a NON-streaming client that never hangs up (the benchmark harness waiting for its response, mode `--mode hold`) the server is still up 40 s after SIGINT and exits 1.2 s after the client closes the connection. The loop has stopped, so the open response can never complete and the process waits for the connection: upstream's graceful shutdown, not our code. Still unknown: who sent the SIGINT in that one arm (nothing in the logs); not reproduced in 8 later arms and 6 shutdown tests. |
| H97 | PASS (robustness check, no speed claim) | The two-GPU prefill under two server slots: `tools/pascal/g1_np2_smoke.py --peer` (D61 stack + follower, `-np 2`, two concurrent prompts of 12,000 tokens, two rounds). With the MTP draft on, the layer-major pass is never used at `-np 2` (the target gives hidden states to the draft, `embeddings_nextn`; `--spec-mtp-defer`, which lifts that, needs `-np 1`): 'a batch of 16384 tokens takes the ordinary path', 0.2 tok/s on the CPU experts, the same with or without the follower. With the draft off the pass runs: 4 prompts gave split passes of 11,484 tokens at 1,409-1,480 tokens/s (single-GPU passes run at ~730), 7 pass lines (leader + follower), 0 fallbacks to the leader, 0 error lines, GPU 0 7,563 MiB, GPU 1 7,707 MiB; a batch that holds tokens of both sequences declines to the ordinary path as before (not a property of the split). No claim about speed or equality at `-np 2`. |
| H98 | DECODE CRITERION MET (+23.0% at 190k); a prefill regression found (x0.62): H99 | Contexts beyond 98k tokens, which every earlier A-B-B-A stopped at. Text: `build/pascal/long-ctx.txt` (the 100k-token harness text followed by this repository's guide, plan and state documents, about 1.2 MB).
